Benchmarks for complex information seeking

481 evaluation artifacts published in 2025–2026 whose tasks cannot be answered by a single search — each read for four properties: whether steps depend on each other, whether the decomposition is knowable up front, whether several answers can be right, and whether an agent can tell when it has found everything.

481artifacts in the corpus
211dedicated benchmarks
270task sets shipped with a method
444read at full-text depth
3349candidates judged

Complete as-of 2026-09-14. Read in order, or jump to a property.

The corpus at a glance

481 artifacts, all published 2025–2026, each one a benchmark, dataset, task suite, environment or live arena whose tasks cannot be answered by a single search. They split into 211 dedicated evaluation artifacts and 270 task sets shipped alongside a method — a distinction kept throughout, because the second group is built to showcase a technique and its task design answers to that.

What the agent is allowed to search with

Warrant for this number

Because. This is the best-evidenced lens in the whole corpus, and for a structural reason: a tool set is a concrete implementation fact, so papers that mention it at all mention it precisely. Fit strata: 365 explicit / 92 reframed / 24 stretch — a 5% stretch share against 28-32% on the decomposition and coverage flags. 361 of 481 rows sit at the highest confidence, and support is overwhelmingly the paper's own experimental setup (423 rows). It was also read deep: 444 of 481 values come from a paper body, because tool sets live in experimental-setup sections and essentially never in abstracts. Retrieving bodies for the whole ring first is what made this column possible at all.

Unless. unless what was read is the BASELINE's toolkit rather than the benchmark's grant — and for some share of these rows it is. Papers routinely describe the tools their own reference agent was built with, while saying nothing about what a different system would be permitted. The two coincide for sandboxed and fixed-corpus artifacts, where the environment IS the constraint, but they come apart for open-web benchmarks, where 137 artifacts sit and where a competitor may bring its own stack. Read the column as 'what this paper shows an agent using', which is a lower bound on what is allowed, and note that `agent-choice` is recorded separately for the artifacts that explicitly leave tooling open.

481 rows, one per artifact, each with a verbatim span. 444 read from paper bodies, 36 from abstracts, 1 artifact has neither an abstract nor a retrievable body and is recorded as unreadable rather than guessed. Tools use a closed nine-value vocabulary with a free-text field for specific named engines; zero off-vocabulary values survived. Artifacts usually grant several tools, so the tool counts sum above the corpus size.

Warrant for this number

Because. These are the best-evidenced aggregates in the report and the only ones that behave like description rather than interpretation. Fit strata: 383 explicit / 53 rephrased / 19 reframed / 26 stretch — 80% explicit, 26 stretch rows in total — and confidence sits at 318 rows of the highest grade. Support composition is 214 from the authors asserting it about their own design, 201 from the paper’s own experiments, 35 from descriptions of the benchmark itself, 12 from reported benchmark results. A paper naming its own artifact a benchmark, its sources the open web, and its deliverable a report is reporting a fact about itself.

Unless. unless the deliverable-shape distribution is read as a natural taxonomy. It is not: the extraction vocabulary offered six shapes and an escape hatch, and the escape hatch was used for a quarter of the corpus — 121 one-off strings that reading turned into ten further shapes, including forecast, trajectory-graded and mixed-battery. The six original shapes are therefore over-represented relative to a vocabulary designed after seeing the data, and the promoted shapes are recorded with a per-value disposition in the canon map rather than inferred.

481 artifact rows, all abstract-depth: the kind/sources/deliverable/modality of an artifact is stated in abstracts essentially without exception, so no body reading was commissioned for this lens. Deliverable shape is reported after canonicalisation; the pre-canonical values are kept.

How this was performed

Computed over the core ring (481 artifacts) under charter v7. Each artifact was read once and its kind, sources, deliverable shape and modality recorded with a verbatim span behind each value. Deliverable shapes were then normalised against a frozen mapping that gives every raw value an explicit disposition, so the long tail of one-off descriptions is grouped deliberately rather than swept into a catch-all. Limit: these are the artifacts' self-descriptions, not an independent audit.

Q1. Do later steps depend on earlier ones?

Whether a task's steps form a chain — and if so, whether the chain is fixed in advance or chosen from what the agent has already found.

Warrant for this number

Because. This is the best-evidenced of the four properties and the only one where a majority of rows rest on directly stated design. Fit strata: 151 explicit / 25 rephrased / 267 reframed / 38 stretch — the stretch share is 8%, less than a quarter of that carried by the decomposition and coverage flags. Support composition is 319 from the authors asserting it about their own design, 45 from rows where the abstract was silent and the value was read from context, 42 from the paper’s own experiments, 27 from descriptions of the benchmark itself: mostly the authors' own description of their agent loop, which is exactly the right kind of source for this question, because whether step t+1 is conditioned on step t is a property of the workflow the paper itself specifies. The full-text pass settled 75 of these and moved the unsettled share from roughly 30% to 17.7%.

Unless. unless 'adaptive' is being read off the capability of the agent scaffold rather than the demand of the tasks — a benchmark evaluated with a re-planning agent will describe adaptive search whether or not its items require it. The rows cannot separate an artifact that forces adaptation from one that merely permits it, so the adaptive share is an upper bound on task-forced adaptivity.

481 winning rows, one per artifact, each with a verbatim span. 75 read from paper bodies, 406 from abstracts. Confidence spread {'1': 310, '2': 125, '0': 46}.

Across the 481 artifacts in the corpus, 85 (17.7%) could not be settled from the evidence available — a number reported rather than filled in. The full-text pass settled this flag for 75 artifacts that the abstract alone left open.

Evidence depth is not even across the two tiers, so they are reported apart rather than pooled: the dedicated-benchmark tier (211 artifacts) leaves 28 unsettled, the method-shipped-task-set tier (270 artifacts) leaves 57. Only the first tier received full-text reading.

What the sources actually say

Across agents, the common failure is not complete ignorance but imprecise mapping from fact patterns to relevant provisions, case holdings, and local standards, followed by unstable legal qualification.

— $OneMillion-Bench, read from the fulltext · coded adaptive

a dynamic ground-truth pipeline that replays expert tool-call trajectories to regenerate answers consistent with the current environment

— AD-Bench, read from the abstract · coded fixed-chain

solving problems through Visual Expansion (invoking visual tools) and Knowledge Expansion (open-web search)

— Agentic-MME, read from the abstract · coded mixed

How this was performed

Computed over the core ring (481 artifacts) under charter v7, from per-paper extraction with a verbatim evidence span required on every record. Each artifact contributes one coded value for this property; where an abstract left it open and the artifact sits in the dedicated-benchmark tier, the paper body was read and that reading supersedes the abstract. 75 values here come from a body, the rest from an abstract. Limit: the 270 method-shipped task sets were not read at body depth, so their unsettled share is a property of the reading, not of the artifacts.

Q2. Is the decomposition known in advance?

Whether the agent can enumerate its sub-questions before searching, or only discovers them by searching.

Warrant for this number

Because. The full-text pass was decisive here — it settled 99 artifacts and cut the unsettled share from roughly 44% to 24.9% — but the surviving distribution is the least directly evidenced of the four. Fit strata: 92 explicit / 10 rephrased / 243 reframed / 136 stretch; 136 rows (28%) are stretch fit and 105 sit at confidence 0. Support composition is 305 from the authors asserting it about their own design, 76 from rows where the abstract was silent and the value was read from context, 25 from descriptions of the benchmark itself, 24 from the paper’s own experiments — dominated by position assertions rather than anything the papers measured. The three-way split should therefore be read as a reasonable reading of how these benchmarks describe themselves, not as a measurement.

Unless. unless the question 'known upfront' is being answered about the wrong party — and in a readable minority of rows it is. The extraction sometimes coded enumerable-at-start because the required evidence chain was fixed BY CONSTRUCTION and therefore known to the benchmark's designer, while the solving agent still cannot see it from the question text; one row says so in terms, noting the chain is 'fixed and knowable by construction (to the benchmark), even if not visible to the solving agent'. A second, related conflation codes a fixed decompose-retrieve-synthesise PIPELINE as an enumerable decomposition, though the sub-questions that pipeline emits are generated per query. Both push mass toward enumerable-at-start, so that category is the soft one and the emergent categories are, if anything, undercounted.

481 winning rows. 99 from bodies, 382 from abstracts. Of the 120 still unsettled, 10 were unsettled even after the body was read — those are genuine silences, not unread papers.

Across the 481 artifacts in the corpus, 120 (24.9%) could not be settled from the evidence available — a number reported rather than filled in. The full-text pass settled this flag for 99 artifacts that the abstract alone left open.

Evidence depth is not even across the two tiers, so they are reported apart rather than pooled: the dedicated-benchmark tier (211 artifacts) leaves 25 unsettled, the method-shipped-task-set tier (270 artifacts) leaves 95. Only the first tier received full-text reading.

What the sources actually say

the benchmark requires retrieving authoritative sources, resolving conflicting evidence, applying domain-specific rules, and making constraint decisions, where correctness depends as much on the reasoning process as the final answer.

— $OneMillion-Bench, read from the fulltext · coded partially-emergent

To mirror a typical scientist’s workflow, the plan’s structure generally follows the logical progression of the paper, from motivation and methods to results and conclusions.

— ADRA-Bank, read from the fulltext · coded enumerable-at-start

fact-checking often involves dependencies across reasoning steps, where the generation of subsequent questions may rely on evidence retrieved from earlier QA pairs.

— AVerImaTeC, read from the fulltext · coded fully-emergent

How this was performed

Computed over the core ring (481 artifacts) under charter v7, from per-paper extraction with a verbatim evidence span required on every record. Each artifact contributes one coded value for this property; where an abstract left it open and the artifact sits in the dedicated-benchmark tier, the paper body was read and that reading supersedes the abstract. 99 values here come from a body, the rest from an abstract. Limit: the 270 method-shipped task sets were not read at body depth, so their unsettled share is a property of the reading, not of the artifacts.

Q3. Are multiple answers possible?

Whether the task has one verifiable answer, a set, a ranking, several defensible answers, or no fixed key at all.

Warrant for this number

Because. This flag has the lowest unsettled share of the four (9.6%) because it is usually decidable from the scoring rule alone: an exact-match metric implies a single key, a set-F1 metric implies a set, a rubric or LLM judge implies several defensible answers. Fit strata: 114 explicit / 67 rephrased / 217 reframed / 83 stretch. Support composition is 353 from the authors asserting it about their own design, 52 from the paper’s own experiments, 35 from descriptions of the benchmark itself, 16 from rows where the abstract was silent and the value was read from context. The single-verifiable half of the corpus is the most solid single number in this report, because a stated exact-match metric is hard to misread.

Unless. unless a stated exact-match metric is masking real multiplicity — several benchmarks score one gold string while their questions admit paraphrases or alternative correct entities. Coding follows the scoring rule, so wherever a benchmark has simply declared one answer correct, this reads as single-verifiable even when the underlying task is not. The single-verifiable share is therefore an upper bound, and the multiple-defensible and open-ended shares are lower bounds.

481 winning rows; only 16 needed the body, because abstracts usually state the metric. Two rows arrived carrying a value from a neighbouring lens's vocabulary and were normalised onto this one; both mappings are recorded with their reasoning in extraction-normalizations.json.

Across the 481 artifacts in the corpus, 46 (9.6%) could not be settled from the evidence available — a number reported rather than filled in. The full-text pass settled this flag for 16 artifacts that the abstract alone left open.

Evidence depth is not even across the two tiers, so they are reported apart rather than pooled: the dedicated-benchmark tier (211 artifacts) leaves 10 unsettled, the method-shipped-task-set tier (270 artifacts) leaves 36. Only the first tier received full-text reading.

jointly measures end-to-end answer correctness (Pass@k) and trajectory coverage

— AD-Bench, read from the abstract · coded single-verifiable

Each artifact contributes one coded value for this property; where an abstract left it open and the artifact sits in the dedicated-benchmark tier, the paper body was read and that reading supersedes the abstract. 16 values here come from a body, the rest from an abstract. Limit: the 270 method-shipped task sets were not read at body depth, so their unsettled share is a property of the reading, not of the artifacts.

Q4. Can the agent tell when it has everything?

Whether a stopping signal exists — a closed set, a stated target count, a partial signal, or nothing reliable.

Warrant for this number

Because. The full-text pass settled 76 artifacts here and cut the unsettled share from roughly 42% to 26.4%. What carries the claim is that a stopping condition is a concrete, checkable thing: a closed answer set, a stated target count, a step budget. Where a paper specifies one, the coding is firm, and this flag draws more own-experiment support than any other (102 rows) because stopping behaviour is something papers actually measure.

Unless. unless the middle category is absorbing the uncertainty of the coder rather than of the agent. The estimable rows are the softest in this report: sampled warrants include 'closed puzzle/game formats typically provide direct feedback' and an inference drawn from the phrase 'decision-making under uncertainty' — genre reasoning and generic difficulty language, not evidence about the artifact's stopping signal. Read hard and knowable as the load-bearing categories and estimable (87) as the soft one. Fit strata overall: 95 explicit / 24 rephrased / 235 reframed / 127 stretch.

481 winning rows, 76 from bodies. Of the 127 still unsettled, only 4 were unsettled after a body was read — the remainder are artifacts whose abstracts were silent and which did not qualify for the full-text pass.

Across the 481 artifacts in the corpus, 127 (26.4%) could not be settled from the evidence available — a number reported rather than filled in. The full-text pass settled this flag for 76 artifacts that the abstract alone left open.

Evidence depth is not even across the two tiers, so they are reported apart rather than pooled: the dedicated-benchmark tier (211 artifacts) leaves 17 unsettled, the method-shipped-task-set tier (270 artifacts) leaves 110. Only the first tier received full-text reading.

What the sources actually say

Rubrics in $OneMillion-Bench are primarily determined case-by-case across different domains and hand-crafted by domain experts.

— $OneMillion-Bench, read from the fulltext · coded hard

a trajectory-aware evaluation that jointly measures end-to-end answer correctness (Pass@k) and trajectory coverage

— AD-Bench, read from the abstract · coded estimable

for research tasks , the source paper itself is designated as the sole piece of gold evidence. For review tasks , the paper’s complete bibliography constitutes the ground-truth evidence set.

— ADRA-Bank, read from the fulltext · coded knowable

How this was performed

Computed over the core ring (481 artifacts) under charter v7, from per-paper extraction with a verbatim evidence span required on every record. Each artifact contributes one coded value for this property; where an abstract left it open and the artifact sits in the dedicated-benchmark tier, the paper body was read and that reading supersedes the abstract. 76 values here come from a body, the rest from an abstract. Limit: the 270 method-shipped task sets were not read at body depth, so their unsettled share is a property of the reading, not of the artifacts.

Q5. What else makes these tasks hard?

The four properties above were specified up front. During extraction each paper was also asked, open-endedly, what else makes its artifact difficult. That produced 1540 free-text phrases over 465 artifacts — no two identical — which were then coded into the families below. 16 artifacts named none and are carried as their own class rather than folded into the unclassifiable tail.

Two denominators are in play and they are not interchangeable: 3.7% of the 1540 phrases resisted classification, while 55 artifacts named at least one such phrase. The first is the codebook's fit; the second is how widely the residue is spread.

Warrant for this number

Because. The open coding is the best-evidenced lens in the extraction — 426 of 481 rows are explicit fit and 350 sit at the highest confidence — because a paper listing what makes its benchmark hard is stating its own design rationale. Every one of the 1,540 phrases was textually unique, so grouping was done by reading each phrase against a codebook derived from this corpus; no keyword rule was used at any stage. The unclassifiable tail came to 3.7% OF PHRASES, well inside the 10% threshold that would have forced the codebook to be re-derived. Measured the other way — artifacts naming at least one unclassifiable property — the residue reaches 55 artifacts, and the report prints both, because a phrase-level fit and an artifact-level spread are different quantities and the smaller one alone would flatter the codebook.

Unless. unless family SIZE is read as evidence of importance in the field. It is not comparable across families: six of the twenty were named as examples in the extraction prompt, and a property a worker was invited to look for is found more often than one it had to notice unaided. Every family therefore ships labelled with its provenance, and only the discovered-in-extraction families carry an unprompted signal. A further caution: these are properties papers CHOSE to foreground, so the distribution tracks what the field currently finds worth claiming novelty for.

481 open-coding rows yielding 1,540 phrases, coded by four independent workers against one frozen codebook. Assignments validated for completeness and vocabulary: no duplicates, no missing phrases, no invalid codes. Families are multi-label — a phrase may carry secondary families — so family counts sum above the artifact count. 16 ring artifacts produced no open-coded phrase at all; they ship as their own class ('no property recorded'), never folded into the unclassifiable tail.

What the sources actually say

a rubric-based evaluation protocol scoring factual accuracy, logical coherence, practical feasibility, and professional compliance, focused on expert-level problems to ensure meaningful differentiation across agents

— $OneMillion-Bench, which names its difficulty as economically consequential, professional-domain framing (Law, Finance, Industry, Healthcare, Natural Science)

a dynamic ground-truth pipeline that replays expert tool-call trajectories to regenerate answers consistent with the current environment, mitigating answer obsolescence

— AD-Bench, which names its difficulty as temporal freshness / ground-truth obsolescence explicitly addressed via a dynamic ground-truth regeneration pipeline

These findings prove that refined training enables medium-sized models to achieve expert-level capabilities at industry-leading cost-efficiency.

— ADR-Bench, which names its difficulty as explicit cost-effectiveness / efficiency goal (medium-sized model matching larger closed-source systems)

(Expected to concentrate in the `relevant` tier, where crit_1==2 method papers live.)76
long-horizon-depthdiscovered-in-extractionDepth as a difficulty: long dependency chains, 100+ tool calls, context-window saturation, long-trace drift and forgetting, error recovery over extended runs. (The depth half of F07's pair.)73
environment-control-and-reproducibilitydiscovered-in-extractionControlling the world so runs are comparable: frozen web snapshots, simulated/sandboxed environments, offline corpora, released harnesses and open recipes.72

temporal-freshnessproposed-by-agentThe answer depends on information that is current at evaluation time — live web, real-time APIs, rolling refresh, forecasting past the knowledge cutoff, ground truth computed at eval time.69
process-level-evaluationdiscovered-in-extractionCredit attaches to the trajectory, not only the final answer: intermediate retrieval quality, standardised action trajectories, stage-wise or progressively-gated scoring, subtask credit.67
adversarial-and-conflicting-sourcesproposed-by-agentThe source space actively fights the agent: poisoned or persuasive evidence, mined hard negatives, distractors, contradictory documents, credibility/authority manipulation, misinformation domains.61
contamination-resistancediscovered-in-extractionConstruction choices whose purpose is to defeat memorisation/leakage: counterfactual or synthetic worlds, on-demand regeneration, entity obfuscation, hint-free design, post-cutoff sourcing. (Distinct from F01: F01 is a task REQUIREMENT, F02 is a construction DEFENCE.)54
answer-completeness-pressurediscovered-in-extractionBeing RIGHT is not enough — the answer must be COMPLETE: recall/precision balance on answer sets, exact-match penalties for omissions, completeness assessment, reconciliation across sources. (Adjacent to user flag (d) coverage_determinability, which measures whether the agent can TELL it is complete; this family is about whether completeness is SCORED. Cross-reference them at report time rather than merging.)48
underspecification-and-personalizationdiscovered-in-extractionThe QUESTION is not fully given: ambiguous or underspecified queries, clarification elicitation, persona/user models, personalised correct answers, implicit contextual cues.47
multilingual-and-cross-lingualdiscovered-in-extractionLanguage as a difficulty or validity axis: bilingual/multilingual task sets, prompt-vs-source language effects, translation-induced validity problems, cultural targeting.41
source-access-limitsproposed-by-agentEvidence that exists but cannot be reached: paywalls, subscriptions, licensing, single-site restriction, open-weights-only or public-data-only constraints.27

Every family carries its provenance: proposed-by-agent families were named as examples in the extraction prompt, so their prominence is partly an artifact of being asked about; discovered-in-extraction families had no prompt cue and emerged from the papers themselves. None of the four headline properties appear here — those were specified by the question.

How this was performed

Open coding over the core ring (481 artifacts), one free-text list per paper, grounded in a verbatim span. Phrases were clustered into a codebook derived from this corpus — never imported — and each phrase was then read and assigned to a family. Because every phrase was textually unique, no keyword rule was used at any point. Limit: the unclassifiable tail is reported as its own labelled class (3.7% of phrases), not folded into a catch-all.

The catalogue — all 481 artifacts

Sortable by any column and filterable by text. Every artifact name links to the paper. What the agent must do is written here in plain words; each opens to show the phrase from the paper it was written from. Search tools lists what the paper says the agent may use — 428 of 481 artifacts state this, and the rest read "not stated", which is recorded rather than guessed.

Learn more Back to arXiv License: CC BY 4.0 arXiv:2505.16582v2 [cs.CL] 26 May 2025 ## O2-Searcher: A Searching-based Agent Model for Open-Domain Open-Ended Question Answering Jianbiao Mei Affiliation:  Zhejiang University Affiliation:  Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory Tao Hu Affiliation:  Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory Affiliation:  University of Science and Technology of China Daocheng Fu Affiliation:  Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory Affiliation:  Fudan University Licheng Wen Affiliation:  Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory Xuemeng Yang Affiliation:  Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory Rong Wu Affiliation:  Zhejiang University Affiliation:  Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory Pinlong Cai Affiliation:  Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory Xinyu Cai Affiliation:  Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory Xing Gao Affiliation:  Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory Yu Yang Affiliation:  Zhejiang University Chengjun Xie Affiliation:  University of Science and Technology of China Botian Shi Affiliation:  Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory Yong Liu Affiliation:  Zhejiang University Affiliation:  State Key Laboratory of Industrial Control Technology Yu Qiao Affiliation:  Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory ## Abstract Large Language Models (LLMs), despite their advancements, are fundamentally limited by their static parametric knowledge, hindering performance on tasks requiring open-domain up-to-date information. While enabling LLMs to interact with external knowledge environments is a promising solution, current efforts primarily address closed-end problems. Open-ended questions, which characterized by lacking a standard answer or providing non-unique and diverse answers, remain underexplored. To bridge this gap, we present O 2 -Searcher, a novel search agent leveraging reinforcement learning to effectively tackle both open-ended and closed-ended questions in the open domain. O 2 -Searcher leverages an efficient, locally simulated search environment for dynamic knowledge acquisition, effectively decoupling the external world knowledge from model’s sophisticated reasoning processes.
local-retrieverweb-search-api
Meilisearch index over a curated, cached web corpus (collected via the Serper API) for open-ended retrieval; an E5-based dense retriever over a locally hosted 2018 Wikipedia dump for closed-ended retrieval; also separately evaluated against a real open-web search environment via the Serper API

How this was performed

One row per artifact in the core ring (481). Publication year and citation count come from Semantic Scholar, read on 2026-09-14 — citation counts move, so that date is part of the number. 480 of 481 artifacts carry a task description; the plain-words summary is written from the paper’s own phrasing, which is shown beside it so the two can be compared. Allowed search tools were read from the paper body wherever one could be retrieved, because tool sets are stated in experimental-setup sections and almost never in abstracts. Every other cell is an extracted value carrying a verbatim span in the shipped data files; an em dash means the source did not settle it. The full table ships as catalog.csv and catalog.json.

Coverage — and what you should not conclude from it

3349 candidates were gathered through several independent routes and every one was judged against the same written criteria; 481 survived into the corpus. The corpus is complete as-of 2026-09-14 for the routes listed below. It will drift as new work appears; the refresh trigger is any new quarter of publication.

Acquisition routeCandidates KeptYield
citation-forward168924014.2%
asta-find90722925.2%
prior-corpus:long-horizon38714136.4%
asta-find-diligent91812113.2%
registry968184.4%
prior-corpus:memory2956221.0%
gap-survey1144438.6%
parametric882730.7%
registry-trec13753.8%
survey-refs1119.1%

Routes overlap — an artifact found twice is counted under both — so the columns do not sum to the corpus size.

Warrant for this number

Because. Routes were chosen to fail differently: registry and parametric enumeration do not pass through the same relevance filter as the query sweeps, so their yields are informative about what the sweeps miss. The retrieval filter's own false-negative rate was measured rather than assumed, by blind-judging a sample of dropped records, and came back at 0%.

Unless. unless the yield table is read as a ranking of route quality. Precision and unique reach are different virtues and they come apart sharply here: the least precise route contributed the largest number of artifacts that no other route found. A route with low yield that reaches a region nothing else reaches is worth more, not less, than its percentage suggests.

Route membership is recorded per artifact, and routes overlap, so the yield columns do not sum to the corpus size. The 19 artifacts whose full text could not be retrieved are carried as a named loss term rather than dropped.

What you should not conclude

  • Not "these are all of them." Curated registries yielded densely, while forward-citation crawling yielded thinly yet surfaced artifacts no other route reached. That pattern is what an incompletely-sampled space looks like, not a saturated one.
  • Not that the flag distributions are properties of the field. They are properties of what 481 papers say about themselves. A benchmark that never discusses its stopping condition is coded unsettled on that property — which is a fact about the paper's reporting.
  • Not that the two tiers are comparable. Only the 211 dedicated artifacts were read at full-text depth; the 270 method-shipped task sets were read at abstract depth only. 19 papers in the deep-reading set had no reachable full text and stayed at abstract depth.
  • Not that unsettled means absent. "Could not be determined" records what the source does not say — never an inference that the property is missing.

Method and package

Candidates were gathered through several deliberately independent routes, then every candidate was judged against written criteria fixed before any judging began. Surviving artifacts were read one at a time and their properties extracted, with a verbatim quotation required behind every coded value — 4114 such records in total. Where an abstract left a property open and the artifact was a dedicated benchmark, the paper body was read and that reading supersedes the abstract; both records are kept, so which depth settled which property stays visible.

Files beside this page: catalog.csv and catalog.json (every artifact, every coded value, every evidence span), flags.json, charts.json, keystats.json, coverage.json. Every number on this page is computed from those files.

Complete as-of 2026-09-14 · corpus of 481 artifacts · refresh trigger: a new quarter of publication. Every artifact name links to its paper.
